Systems of Wave Equations on Asymptotically de Sitter Vacuum Spacetimes in All Even Spatial Dimensions
Serban Cicortas

Abstract
This is the second paper of a two part work that establishes a definitive quantitative nonlinear scattering theory for asymptotically de Sitter vacuum solutions ( ℳ , g ) \big(\mathcal{M},g\big) in ( n + 1 ) (n+1) dimensions with n ≥ 4 n\geq 4 even, which are determined by small scattering data at ℐ ± . \mathcal{I}^{\pm}. In this paper we prove quantitative estimates for systems of wave equations on the ( ℳ , g ) \big(\mathcal{M},g\big) backgrounds. The systems considered include the Einstein vacuum equations commuted with suitable time-dependent vector fields, where we treat the nonlinear terms as general inhomogeneous factors. The estimates obtained are essential in establishing sharp top order estimates in [ Cic24 ] for the scattering map of the Einstein vacuum equations, taking asymptotic data at ℐ − \mathcal{I}^{-} to asymptotic data at ℐ + \mathcal{I}^{+} .



1
Introduction

In this work, we study systems of wave equations on asymptotically de Sitter vacuum backgrounds and we establish quantitative estimates. The simplest setting considered is given by the scalar wave equation on the background of de Sitter space:

\square_{g_{dS}}\phi=0.

More generally, we consider time-dependent background metrics in the class of asymptotically de Sitter vacuum spacetimes, and we allow for inhomogeneous terms on the right hand side of the equations.

We apply the results of this paper in
[
Cic24
]
for the Einstein vacuum equations with a positive cosmological constant, which can be written as a system of wave equations with the nonlinear terms treated as general inhomogeneous factors. We note that the current work also generalizes our previous results in
[
Cic23
]
, where we established a scattering theory for the wave equation on exact de Sitter space.

We define below asymptotically de Sitter vacuum solutions, which represent the background spacetimes for the systems of wave equations considered in this paper. We state the main results in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
. We then explain the relation of the main theorems to the nonlinear scattering theory proved in
[
Cic24
]
.

Asymptotically de Sitter vacuum solutions.

We consider the
(n+1)
-dimensional Einstein vacuum equations with positive cosmological constant
\Lambda=\frac{n(n-1)}{2},
for any
n\geq 4
even:

Ric_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}+\Lambda g_{\mu\nu}=0.

(1.1)

We consider asymptotically de Sitter solutions of (
1.1
) arising from scattering data at past infinity
\mathcal{I}^{-}
. These are vacuum spacetimes
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
of the form
\mathcal{M}=(0,\tau_{0})\times S^{n}
for some
\tau_{0}>0,
which can be written with respect to coordinates
\{\theta^{A}\}
associated to an arbitrary chart on
S^{n}
as follows:

g=-\frac{d\tau^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+\frac{1}{4\tau^{2}}\not{g}_{AB}(\tau,\theta^{1},\ldots,\theta^{n})d\theta^{A}d\theta^{B}.

(1.2)

Moreover, we also require that the spacetime
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
is determined by scattering data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}=\{\tau=0\}
consisting of a Riemannian metric
\big(S^{n},\not{g}_{0}\big)
and a symmetric traceless 2-tensor
h
on
S^{n}
, which satisfies additional constraints.

The standard example of an asymptotically de Sitter vacuum solution is given by the
(n+1)
-dimensional de Sitter space
\big((0,\infty)\times S^{n},g_{dS}\big)
. Denoting by
\not{g}_{S^{n}}
the round metric on
S^{n},
we have:

g_{dS}=-\frac{d\tau^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+\frac{1}{4\tau^{2}}\not{g}_{dS}=-\frac{d\tau^{2}}{\tau^{2}}+\frac{1}{4\tau^{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}+2\tau^{2}\bigg)^{2}\not{g}_{S^{n}}.

(1.3)

\{\tau=1\}\cong S^{n}
\mathcal{I}^{-}=\{\tau\rightarrow 0\}\cong S^{n}
\mathcal{I}^{+}=\{\tau\rightarrow\infty\}\cong S^{n}

Figure 1:
Diagram of the the
(n+1)
-dimensional de Sitter space
\big((0,\infty)\times S^{n},g_{dS}\big)
.

According to the results in
[
FG85
,
FG12
]
,
[
RSR18
]
, and
[
Hin24
]
, we have that for any
n\geq 4
even and any smooth scattering data
\big(\not{g}_{0},h\big)
there exists a unique asymptotically de Sitter vacuum solution satisfying the expansion at
\tau=0
:

\not{g}=\not{g}_{0}+\tau^{2}\not{g}_{1}+\ldots+\frac{\tau^{n-2}}{(n/2-1)!}\not{g}_{n/2-1}+\frac{2\tau^{n}\log(2\tau)}{(n/2)!}\mathcal{O}+\frac{\tau^{n}}{(n/2)!}k+O\big(\tau^{n+2}|\log\tau|\big),

(1.4)

where the tensors
\not{g}_{1},\ldots,\not{g}_{n/2-1},\mathcal{O},\text{tr}k
are determined by
\not{g}_{0}
through certain compatibility relations,
h
is the trace free part of
k,
and the higher order terms in the expansion are determined by
\not{g}_{0}
and
h
.

An essential feature that creates significant challenges in our work is the lack of smoothness in
\tau
of the expansion (
1.4
) due to the term
\mathcal{O},
called the obstruction tensor. On the other hand, in the case of odd spatial dimension
n
the corresponding expansion is smooth in
\tau,
which results in considerable simplifications.

Assumptions on the background spacetime.

Before introducing the systems of wave equations and stating the main results, we fix the assumptions made on the background spacetime
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
for the rest of the paper.

We fix
\epsilon>0
to be small enough and
N>0
to be a large integer depending on
n
. We consider
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
to be a smooth asymptotically de Sitter vacuum solution of the form (
1.2
) determined by scattering data
\big(\not{g}_{0},h\big)
, with
\mathcal{M}=(0,1]\times S^{n}.

1
1

1

In the context of
[
Cic24
]
, we will always work with globally defined spacetimes with
\mathcal{M}=(0,\infty)\times S^{n}
which are quantitatively close to de Sitter space. However, in view of the time inversion transformation
\tau\rightarrow 1/\tau
, it suffices to restrict to
\mathcal{M}=(0,1]\times S^{n}
for the purpose of this paper.
We assume that
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
is close to de Sitter space, in the sense that:

\sum_{i=0}^{N}\sup_{\mathcal{M}}\big|\mathcal{L}_{\theta}^{i}\big(\not{g}-\not{g}_{dS}\big)\big|\leq\epsilon,

(1.5)

where
\not{g}_{dS}
is defined in (
1.3
), we denote by
\mathcal{L}_{\theta}^{i}
all the combinations of
i
Lie angular derivatives in a coordinate chart, and we sum over a family of coordinate charts that covers
S^{n}.

We use the convention that the absolute value
|\cdot|
, the integration volume form, and the covariant angular derivatives are taken with respect to the conformal metric
\not{g}
induced on
S_{\tau}=\{\tau\}\times S^{n}
. We also denote by
H^{k}(S_{\tau})
the
L^{2}
-based Sobolev space of order
k
defined with respect
\not{g}
, which is defined in detail in Section
2
.

We denote by
\chi=\mathcal{L}_{e_{4}}\not{g}
the renormalized second fundamental form of
S_{\tau}
with
e_{4}=\frac{1}{2\tau}\partial_{\tau},
and we denote the schematic quantities
\psi\in\{1,\not{g},\chi,\tau^{2}\chi\}
. We assume that for some
C_{0}>0
we have the bound:

\sup_{\tau\in(0,1]}\big\|\chi\big\|_{H^{N}(S_{\tau})}+\sup_{\tau\in(0,1]}\big\|\nabla_{\tau}\chi\big\|_{H^{N}(S_{\tau})}\leq C_{0}.

(1.6)

Finally, we say that a tensor is horizontal on
\mathcal{M}
if its entries are in
TS_{\tau}
.

The model systems.

We introduce two model systems of wave equations on the
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
background. These are motivated by
[
Cic24
, Section 5]
and they include the Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
) commuted
n/2
times with the vector field
e_{4}=\frac{1}{2\tau}\partial_{\tau}.
According to the expansion (
1.4
), commuting at this order captures the singular behavior of the solution caused by the obstruction tensor
\mathcal{O}.

Definition 1.1

(Model Systems)
.

We fix the integers
I>0
and
\sigma\in\{1,2\}.
Let
\Phi_{0},\ldots,\Phi_{I}
be smooth horizontal tensors on
\mathcal{M}
, which satisfy the expansions at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
for all
1\leq i\leq I
:

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}=2\mathcal{O}\log\tau+h+O\big(\tau^{2}|\log\tau|^{2}\big),\ \nabla_{\tau}\Phi_{0}=\frac{2\mathcal{O}}{\tau}+O\big(\tau|\log\tau|^{2}\big)\text{ in }C^{\infty}(S^{n})

\displaystyle\Phi_{i}=\Phi_{i}^{0}+O\big(\tau^{2}|\log\tau|^{2}\big),\ \nabla_{\tau}\Phi_{i}=O\big(\tau|\log\tau|^{2}\big)\text{ in }C^{\infty}(S^{n}).

Based on these expansions, we say that
\Phi_{0}
is a singular quantity and that
\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{I}
are regular quantities at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.

We assume that
\Phi_{0},\ldots,\Phi_{I}
satisfy the model system of wave equations for any
0\leq m\leq M
:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)+\frac{1}{\tau}\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}-4\Delta_{\not{g}}\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}=\psi\nabla^{m+1}\Phi+F_{m}^{0}

(1.7)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{m}\Phi_{i}\big)+(-1)^{\sigma-1}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau}\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{m}\Phi_{i}-4\Delta_{\not{g}}\nabla^{m}\Phi_{i}=\sum_{j=\sigma-1}^{I}\psi\nabla^{m+1}\Phi_{j}+F_{m}^{i},

(1.8)

where
\psi\nabla^{m+1}\Phi
is a schematic notation for sums of general terms with any
\psi\in\{1,\not{g},\chi,\tau^{2}\chi\}
,
\Phi\in\{\Phi_{0},\ldots,\Phi_{I}\}
, the inhomogeneous terms satisfy
F_{m}^{0},F_{m}^{i}\in L^{1}_{\tau}\big([0,1]\big)C^{\infty}(S^{n}),
and the covariant angular derivatives
\nabla
are taken with respect to the conformal metric
\not{g}
induced on
S_{\tau}
according to (
1.2
).

We refer to the above system with the choice of sign
\sigma=1
as the
first model system
, and similarly in the case
\sigma=2
as the
second model system
.

An additional motivation for the model systems which does not rely on the computations for the Einstein equations is provided by the wave equation on asymptotically de Sitter spacetimes. Let
\phi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth solution of:

\square_{g}\phi=0.

(1.9)

According to
[
Vas10
]
,
\phi
satisfies a similar expansion to (
1.4
). Commuting the wave equation up to
n/2
times with the vector field
e_{4}
, we set
\Phi_{0}=(e_{4})^{n/2}\phi,

\Phi_{i}=(e_{4})^{n/2-i}\phi,
for
1\leq i\leq I=n/2,
and we obtain that
\big(\Phi_{0},\ldots,\Phi_{I}\big)
satisfy both model systems. In particular, this contains the case of the wave equation on exact de Sitter space considered in our previous work
[
Cic23
]
.

As in the case of the above wave equation example, we prove in
[
Cic24
, Section 5]
that the commuted Einstein vacuum equations also satisfy both model systems with suitable inhomogeneous terms. We consider two model systems since in the case of the regular quantities
\nabla^{m}\Phi_{i}
we have flexibility on which equations to use when proving estimates. On the other hand, we notice that for the singular quantities
\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}
we always work with (
1.7
).

The main results.

We prove quantitative estimates for the two model systems on the background spacetime
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
. In order to obtain sharp estimates, we consider a renormalization of the tensor
h
in the expansion of
\Phi_{0}:

\mathfrak{h}=h-2(\log\nabla)\mathcal{O},

(1.10)

where the operator
\log\nabla
is defined in Section
2
using the geometric Littlewood-Paley theory of
[
KR06
]
. We note that the need to renormalize
h
is already present in the simplified model problem considered in
[
Cic23
]
for the scattering for the linear wave equation on exact de Sitter space.

For solutions of the first model system, we prove estimates at
\tau\in(0,1]
in terms of the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
:

Theorem 1.1

(First model system)
.

Let
M>N
be large enough. We assume that
\Phi_{0},\ldots,\Phi_{I}
satisfy the first model system on the background
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big),
with
\sigma=1
in (
1.8
). We also assume that the sphere
\big(S^{n},\not{g}_{0}\big)
satisfies for some
C_{1}>0:

\big\|\not{Riem}(\not{g}_{0})\big\|_{H^{M}(S^{n})}\leq C_{1}.

For all
\tau\in(0,1],
we define the energy of the solution on
S_{\tau}
to be:

\mathcal{E}_{I}(\tau)=\tau^{2}\big\|\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}}+\tau^{2}\big\|\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big\|^{2}_{H^{3/2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\Big(\tau\big\|\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{i}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}}+\tau\big\|\nabla^{M+1}\Phi_{i}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}}+\big\|\Phi_{i}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}}\Big).

We define the asymptotic data norm and the inhomogeneous norm as:

\mathcal{D}_{I}=\big\|\mathcal{O}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}}+\big\|\mathfrak{h}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|\Phi_{i}^{0}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}},\ \mathcal{F}_{I}(\tau)=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sum_{i=0}^{I}\int_{0}^{\tau}\big\|F_{m}^{i}\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}d\tau^{\prime}+\sum_{i=0}^{I}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tau^{\prime}\big\|F_{M}^{i}\big\|_{H^{1/2}}^{2}d\tau^{\prime}.

The solution of the first model system satisfies the estimates for some constant
C_{I}=C_{I}\big(M,C_{0},C_{1}\big)>0
:

\mathcal{E}_{I}(\tau)\leq C_{I}\mathcal{D}_{I}+C_{I}\mathcal{F}_{I}(\tau),

(1.11)

\big\|\Phi_{0}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}}\leq C_{I}\mathcal{D}_{I}+C_{I}\mathcal{F}_{I}(\tau)+C_{I}|\log\tau|^{2}\big\|\mathcal{O}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}}.

(1.12)

For solutions of the second model system, we prove estimates at
\tau\in(0,1)
in terms of the solution at
\tau=1
. We also prove estimates for the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
.

Theorem 1.2

(Second model system)
.

Let
M>N
be large enough. We assume that
\Phi_{0},\ldots,\Phi_{I}
satisfy the second model system on the background
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big),
with
\sigma=2
in (
1.8
). We also assume that on
S_{1}=\{1\}\times S^{n}
we have for some
C_{2}>0:

\big\|\psi\big\|_{H^{M+1}(S_{1})}\leq C_{2}.

For all
\tau\in(0,1],
we denote by
\mathcal{E}_{II}(\tau)
the energy of the solution on
S_{\tau}
, which is defined in detail in (
4
). We have schematically at top order with respect to angular derivatives:

\mathcal{E}_{II}(\tau)=\tau^{2}\big\|\Phi_{0}\big\|_{H^{M+3/2}}^{2}+\tau^{2}\big\|\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big\|_{H^{1/2}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|\Phi_{i}\big\|_{H^{M+3/2}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{i}\big\|_{H^{1/2}}^{2}+\ldots

We define the asymptotic data norm and the inhomogeneous norm as:

\mathcal{D}_{II}=\sum_{i=0}^{I}\big\|\Phi_{i}\big\|_{H^{M+3/2}(S_{1})}^{2}+\sum_{i=0}^{I}\big\|\nabla_{\tau}\Phi_{i}\big\|_{H^{M+1/2}(S_{1})}^{2},\ \mathcal{F}_{II}(\tau)=\sum_{i=0}^{I}\sum_{m=0}^{M}\int_{\tau}^{1}\tau^{\prime}\big\|F_{m}^{i}\big\|_{H^{1/2}}^{2}d\tau^{\prime}.

The solution of the second model system satisfies the estimate for some constant
C_{II}=C_{II}\big(M,C_{0},C_{2}\big)>0
:

\mathcal{E}_{II}(\tau)\leq C_{II}\mathcal{D}_{II}+C_{II}\mathcal{F}_{II}(\tau).

(1.13)

Moreover, the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
given by
\Phi_{i}^{0},\ \mathcal{O},
and
\mathfrak{h}
satisfies the estimates:

\big\|\mathcal{O}\big\|_{H^{M+1}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|\Phi_{i}^{0}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}}\leq C_{II}\mathcal{D}_{II}+C_{II}\mathcal{F}_{II}(0),

(1.14)

\big\|\mathfrak{h}\big\|_{H^{M+1}}^{2}\leq C\Big(C_{II},\big\|\not{Riem}(\not{g}_{0})\big\|_{H^{M}}\Big)\cdot\Big(\mathcal{D}_{II}+\mathcal{F}_{II}(0)+\big\|h\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big).

(1.15)

Remark 1.1
.

Solutions of the wave equation (
1.9
) on the background
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
satisfy both model systems, where we set
\Phi_{0}=(e_{4})^{n/2}\phi,

\Phi_{i}=(e_{4})^{n/2-i}\phi,
for
1\leq i\leq I=n/2.
In particular, Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
generalize our previous results in
[
Cic23
]
, where we proved a scattering theory for the wave equation on exact de Sitter space. We use
[
Cic23
]
as a guideline for the current paper.

Remark 1.2
.

For both model systems, the solution at
\tau=1
and the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
naturally lie in different Sobolev spaces. In particular, solutions of the first model system "gain"
1/2
derivatives at
\tau=1
compared to the asymptotic data. Similarly, the asymptotic data of solutions of the second model system "lose"
1/2
derivatives compared to the solution at
\tau=1
. This behavior is already present at the level of the linear wave equation on exact de Sitter space in
[
Cic23
]
, and is explained below using the asymptotics of the Bessel functions in Section
1.1.1
. We also point out that capturing this structure of the solutions, together with the need to renormalize
h
to
\mathfrak{h}
, requires decomposing the solutions using the geometric LP theory of
[
KR06
]
.

Remark 1.3
.

The first model system has a favorable structure for "forward" estimates, which consist of proving bounds for the solution at times
\tau\in(0,1]
in terms of the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
. On the other hand, the second model system is more suitable for "backward" estimates, which consist of proving bounds for the solution at times
\tau\in(0,1)
and for the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
in terms of the solution at
\tau=1
. While the equation (
1.7
) for the singular quantity
\Phi_{0}
is the same for both model systems, the flexibility in (
1.8
) simplifies our analysis of the quantities that are regular at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
. Our approach of using the model systems to prove sharp estimates for the scattering map in
[
Cic24
]
is justified since the commuted Einstein vacuum equations satisfy both model systems. Moreover, we defined the norms in order to have
\mathcal{E}_{I}(1)\sim\mathcal{D}_{II},
which allows us to combine the estimates for the two model systems in
[
Cic24
]
.

The nonlinear scattering theory.

In
[
Cic24
]
, we established a definitive quantitative nonlinear scattering theory for asymptotically de Sitter solutions of the Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
), which are determined by small scattering data at
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}
. We present this result and explain how it relies on the Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
proved in the present paper.

We introduce briefly some notation, and refer the reader to
[
Cic24
]
for precise definitions. Given smooth scattering data
\big(\not{g}_{0},h\big),
we denote by
\Sigma\big(\not{g}_{0},h\big)
an asymptotic initial data set consisting of certain tensors that can be computed in terms of
\not{g}_{0},h,
and their derivatives. We note that two essential components of
\Sigma\big(\not{g}_{0},h\big)
are the obstruction tensor
\mathcal{O}
and the renormalized tensor
\mathfrak{h}=h-2\big(\log\nabla\big)\mathcal{O}.
In particular, we denote by
\Sigma_{dS}
the asymptotic initial data set corresponding to de Sitter space. For any
M>0
, we define the asymptotic initial data norm
\big\|\Sigma\big(\not{g}_{0},h\big)\big\|_{M}
to be an
L^{2}
-based Sobolev type norm of order
M
measuring closeness to the de Sitter data. For any
\varepsilon>0
, we define
B_{\varepsilon}^{M}\big(\Sigma_{dS}\big)
to be the set of smooth
\varepsilon
-small asymptotic data of order
M
, given by the open ball of size
\varepsilon
around
\Sigma_{dS}
with respect to the asymptotic initial data norm.

We state the main result of
[
Cic24
]
:

Theorem 1.3

(
[
Cic24
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

For any even integer
n\geq 4,
we have a scattering theory for asymptotically de Sitter vacuum solutions determined by small data. For any
M>0
large enough there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
small enough, such that for any
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
we have:

1.

Existence and uniqueness of scattering states: for any
\varepsilon
-small asymptotic data of order
M
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
or
\mathcal{I}^{+}
given by
\Sigma\big(\not{g}_{0},h\big)\in B_{\varepsilon}^{M}\big(\Sigma_{dS}\big)
, there exists a unique smooth global solution
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
of the form (
1.2
) to the Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
) which remains quantitatively close to the de Sitter metric;

2.

Asymptotic completeness: any smooth solution of the Einstein vacuum equations (
1.1
) of the form (
1.2
), which is quantitatively close to the de Sitter metric at a finite time
\tau,
exists globally and induces scattering data
\big(\not{g}_{0},h\big)
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
and
\big(\underline{\not{g}_{0}},\underline{h}\big)
at
\mathcal{I}^{+};

3.

Existence of a scattering map with quantitative estimates: there exists a constant
C_{M}>0
independent of
\varepsilon,
such that we have a well-defined scattering map taking the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
to asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{+}:

\mathcal{S}:B_{\varepsilon}^{M}\big(\Sigma_{dS}\big)\rightarrow B_{C_{M}\varepsilon}^{M}\big(\Sigma_{dS}\big),\ \mathcal{S}\Big(\Sigma\big(\not{g}_{0},h\big)\Big)=\Sigma\big(\underline{\not{g}_{0}},\underline{h}\big).

(1.16)

The scattering map is locally invertible and locally Lipschitz at
\Sigma_{dS},
satisfying the quantitative estimate:

\Big\|\mathcal{S}\big(\Sigma(\not{g}_{0},h)\big)\Big\|_{M}\leq C_{M}\Big\|\Sigma\big(\not{g}_{0},h\big)\Big\|_{M}.

(1.17)

This estimate is sharp and avoids any "derivative loss", in the sense that we use the same Sobolev-type norm of order
M
to measure the smallness of asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}.

The first two statements of Theorem
1.3
are proved entirely in
[
Cic24
]
. In particular, given small scattering data we obtain a global smooth solution
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
with
\mathcal{M}=(0,\infty)\times S^{n}
, which is quantitatively close to the de Sitter metric. We use these bounds as preliminary estimates in order to justify our assumptions (
1.5
) and (
1.6
).

The construction of the scattering map given by (
1.16
) and the sharp estimate (
1.17
) rely on
[
Cic24
, Theorem 7.1, Theorem 9.1, Theorem 9.2]
, which follow from Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
proved in the present paper. We treat the Einstein equations (
1.1
) commuted with
e_{4}
at top order using the model systems on the
\big(\mathcal{M},g\big)
background, with the nonlinear terms contained in the inhomogeneous terms. Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
give sharp estimates for the top order quantities, which are then used in
[
Cic24
]
to deal with the nonlinear terms and ultimately prove (
1.17
). We note that the main estimates proved in the present paper are also illustrated in
[
Cic24
, Section 7, Section 9]
for a simplified toy problem.

We outline the structure of the remainder of the introduction. In Section
1.1
, we present the main steps of the proof. We illustrate the argument of
[
Cic23
]
for a toy problem in Section
1.1.1
. We then introduce the geometric Littlewood-Paley theory of
[
KR06
]
in Section
1.1.2
. In Section
1.1.3
, we outline the proof of Theorem
1.1
. In Section
1.1.4
, we explain the main steps in the proof of Theorem
1.2
. Finally, we outline the structure of the rest of the paper in Section
1.2
.

1.1
Outline of the proof

1.1.1
A toy problem on the background of de Sitter

We consider a toy problem for the model systems on the background of exact de Sitter space. This contains some important features of the general problem, and it allows us to illustrate the argument of
[
Cic23
]
.

We consider
\alpha:(0,1]\times S^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
to be a solution of:

\partial_{\tau}^{2}\alpha+\frac{1}{\tau}\partial_{\tau}\alpha-\Delta_{\not{g}_{S^{n}}}\alpha=f_{1}(\tau)\tau\partial_{\tau}\alpha+f_{2}(\tau)\alpha

(1.18)

\alpha=\alpha_{\mathcal{O}}\log\tau+\alpha_{h}+O\big(\tau^{2}|\log\tau|^{2}\big),

where
|f_{1}|+|f_{2}|=O(1)
. Moreover, we assume that
\alpha
is supported on spherical harmonics with corresponding eigenvalues in
[2^{2l},2^{2l+2}).
We introduce the new time variable
t=2^{l}\tau
and decompose the solution as
\alpha=\alpha_{J}+\alpha_{Y},
where
\alpha_{J},\ \alpha_{Y}
solve (
1.18
), and satisfy the expansions:

\alpha_{J}=\alpha_{\mathfrak{h}}+O\big(t^{2}|\log t|^{2}\big),\ \alpha_{Y}=\alpha_{\mathcal{O}}\log t+O\big(t^{2}|\log t|^{2}\big),

with
\alpha_{\mathfrak{h}}=\alpha_{h}-\alpha_{\mathcal{O}}\log 2^{l}.
We refer to
\alpha_{J},\ \alpha_{Y}
as the regular and singular components of
\alpha.

As suggested by our notation,
\alpha_{J}
and
\alpha_{Y}
satisfy similar bounds in terms of
t
to the first and second Bessel functions
J_{0},\ Y_{0}
. Thus, at
t=0
we have
|\alpha_{J}|\lesssim 1,\ |\alpha_{Y}|\lesssim|\log t|,
while at
t=2^{l}
we have
|\alpha_{J}|,|\alpha_{Y}|\lesssim t^{-1/2}.
The asymptotic behavior at
t=0
implies the need to renormalize
\alpha_{h}
to
\alpha_{\mathfrak{h}},
similarly to (
1.10
). Moreover, the asymptotic behavior of the Bessel functions for large
t
implies that at
\tau=1
we have
\alpha_{J},\alpha_{Y}\sim 2^{-l/2}.
This determines the "gain" of 1/2 derivatives for the solution at
\tau=1
compared to the asymptotic data at
\tau=0,
similarly to Remark
1.2
.

While the above discussion provides heuristics on the behavior of the solution, we note that the results of
[
Cic23
]
do not rely on the theory of Bessel functions. Instead, we capture the quantitative properties of the solution by constructing frequency dependent multipliers and proving energy estimates separately in the low frequency regime
t\in(0,1]
and in the high frequency regime
t\in[1,2^{l}].
We explain this in detail below for the model systems.

1.1.2
Geometric Littlewood-Paley projections

According to Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, a remarkable property of solutions of the model systems is that the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
and the solution at
\{\tau=1\}
lie in Sobolev spaces which differ by 1/2 in terms of regularity. The detailed analysis needed to capture this property requires the use of Littlewood-Paley projections. These are used to define fractional Sobolev spaces, the
\log\nabla
operator present in the renormalization of
h,
and to construct the frequency dependent multipliers needed in our estimates.

In the case of the toy problem considered above, we projected using the spherical harmonics decomposition. However, in the general setting the conformal metric
\not{g}
induced by the background on the spheres
S_{\tau}
has a nontrivial time dependence. As a result, we use the geometric Littlewood-Paley theory of Klainerman-Rodnianski from
[
KR06
]
, which we introduce in detail in Section
2
. We also use the methods of
[
KR06
,
KR05
]
to prove additional new results that are needed in our situation, which we outline below.

The geometric LP projections satisfy a series of standard properties, which in particular allow us to define fractional derivatives. For example, the operator
\log\nabla
used in the renormalization (
1.10
) is defined as:

(\log\nabla)F=\sum_{k\geq 0}P_{k}^{2}F\cdot\log 2^{k}.

Unlike the case of the toy problem, we encounter additional difficulties since the LP projections only satisfy
L^{2}
-almost orthogonality. For any two families of LP projections
P_{k}
and
P^{\prime}_{l}
we have according to
[
KR06
]
:

\big\|P_{k}P^{\prime}_{l}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{-4|k-l|}\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

The LP projections satisfy the finite band property
\|P_{k}F\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{-k}\|\nabla\widetilde{P}_{k}F\|_{L^{2}},
where
\widetilde{P}_{k}^{2}=P_{k}.
Because of the
L^{2}
-almost orthogonality, bounding the RHS of this inequality using the
P_{l}
projection operators creates dangerous terms with frequency higher than
k.
Instead, we prove a novel refined Poincaré inequality for any
k\geq 0
and
\delta>0:

\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta}2^{-2k}\big\|\nabla P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\delta\sum_{0\leq l<k}2^{-9k+7l}\big\|\nabla P_{l}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\delta^{-1}2^{-4k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(1.19)

The important feature of this inequality is that all the LP projection operators have the same symbol. Moreover, only the last term contains frequencies higher than
k
, but this is lower order due to the good
2^{-4k}
weight.

Finally, we also prove bounds for the commutation error terms obtained due to the time dependence of the metric
\not{g}.
For example, for a certain projection operator
\underline{\widetilde{P}}_{k}
defined in Section
2
in terms of
P_{k}
, we have:

\big\|[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\big\|\underline{\widetilde{P}}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}+2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

(1.20)

While the presence of projection operators with different symbols in this inequality cannot be avoided, we point out that both sides of the inequality can be summed for
k\geq 0,
which will prove to be essential later.

1.1.3
The proof of Theorem
1.1

The goal of Theorem
1.1
is to obtain estimates for solutions of the first model system at
\tau\in(0,1]
in terms of the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}.
We provide a detailed outline of the proof of Theorem
1.1
, and note the reader should use this section for assistance when reading the proof in Section
3
. Our strategy is to adapt the approach in
[
Cic23
]
to the current setting by using the geometric LP theory instead. We first decompose the solution into its singular and regular parts, which we treat separately in all the estimates. We then explain how to prove lower order estimates and top order estimates, which complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Decomposition of
\Phi_{0}
.

The first model system has a favorable structure for proving estimates for the quantities
\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{I}
in terms of the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-},
due to the choice of sign
\sigma=1
in Definition
1.1
. Moreover, these quantities are regular at
\tau=0,
according to their asymptotic expansions. While the equation for
\Phi_{0}
has the same favorable structure, we encounter difficulties since
\Phi_{0}
blows-up at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
as
\log\tau.
We isolate this singular behavior by defining the singular component of
\Phi_{0}
, which decouples from the rest of the system and can be treated separately. The remaining component of
\Phi_{0}
is regular at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
and can be treated similarly to the other regular quantities.

For each
m\leq M,
we define the singular component
\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}
to be the horizontal tensor solving:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}\big)+\frac{1}{\tau}\nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}-4\Delta\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}=\psi\nabla\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}

(1.21)

\displaystyle\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}({\tau})=2\nabla^{m}\mathcal{O}\log({\tau})+2(\log\nabla)\nabla^{m}\mathcal{O}+O\big({\tau}^{2}|\log({\tau})|^{2}\big),\ \nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}({\tau})=\frac{2\nabla^{m}\mathcal{O}}{\tau}+O\big({\tau}|\log({\tau})|^{2}\big).

We prove the existence and uniqueness of the singular component in Section
3.1
. We define the regular component:

\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}=\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}-\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}.

Making the renormalization
\mathfrak{h}_{m}=\nabla^{m}h-2(\log\nabla)\nabla^{m}\mathcal{O},
we get that the regular component satisfies the equation:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}\big)+\frac{1}{\tau}\nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}-4\Delta\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}=\psi\nabla\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}+\sum_{j=1}^{I}\psi\nabla^{m+1}\Phi_{j}+F_{m}^{0}

(1.22)

\displaystyle\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}({\tau})=\mathfrak{h}_{m}+O\big({\tau}^{2}|\log({\tau})|^{2}\big),\ \nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}({\tau})=O\big({\tau}|\log({\tau})|^{2}\big)\text{ in }C^{\infty}(S^{n}).

Lower order estimates.

In order to prove the needed refined estimates for the solution at top order, it is essential to first prove in Section
3.2
energy estimates that are lower order in terms of angular derivatives. We treat separately the singular and regular quantities. For the regular quantities we note that the favorable choice of sign
\sigma=1
and the regularity at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
allow us to use
\nabla_{\tau}
as a multiplier to prove in Proposition
3.3
:

\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\big\|\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|\Phi_{i}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M}}\leq C_{I}\mathcal{D}_{I}+C_{I}\mathcal{F}_{I}(\tau).

(1.23)

For the singular component, the lower order version of (
1.12
) consists of proving in Proposition
3.2
:

\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\big\|\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}\leq C_{I}\mathcal{D}_{I}+C_{I}|\log\tau|^{2}\big\|\mathcal{O}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}}.

(1.24)

To prove this, we further decompose for every
m<M:

\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}=\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}^{1}+\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}^{2},
where
\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}^{1}
and
\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}^{2}
are the solutions of (
1.21
) that satisfy the expansions:

\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}^{1}({\tau})=2\nabla^{m}\mathcal{O}\log({\tau})+O\big({\tau}^{2}|\log({\tau})|^{2}\big),\ \big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}^{2}({\tau})=2(\log\nabla)\nabla^{m}\mathcal{O}+O\big({\tau}^{2}|\log({\tau})|^{2}\big).

The desired lower order estimate for the singular component follows using the
\nabla_{\tau}
multiplier in the equations for
\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}^{1}/\log\tau
and
\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}^{2},
similarly to the approach in
[
Cic23
]
.

Additionally, we prove estimates for the commutator term
\mathcal{C}=\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}-\nabla\big(\nabla^{M-1}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}
and
H^{1/2}
estimates for
\nabla(\nabla^{M-1}\Phi_{0})_{Y}
and
\nabla_{\tau}(\nabla^{M-1}\Phi_{0})_{Y}
in Section
3.2.2
, which simplify the proof of the top order estimates.

Top order estimates for the regular components.

In order to capture the specific behavior of the regular components at top order, we consider the equations satisfied by the projections
P_{k}\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}
and
P_{k}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{i}
, for all
1\leq i\leq I
and
k\geq 0.
The goal is to prove similar estimates to the ones for the toy problem in Section
1.1.1
, dictated by the asymptotics of the Bessel function
J_{0}.

For each
P_{k}
projection we consider the low frequency regime
\tau\in(0,2^{-k-1}]
in Section
3.3.1
. We propagate the
L^{2}
bounds satisfied by the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
using
\nabla_{\tau}
as a multiplier to obtain schematically for
\tau\in(0,2^{-k-1}]
:

\big\|P_{k}\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|P_{k}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{i}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\ldots\lesssim\big\|P_{k}\mathfrak{h}_{M}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|P_{k}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{i}^{0}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\sum_{i=0}^{I}\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{1}{2^{k}}\big\|P_{k}F_{M}^{i}\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}d\tau^{\prime}+\ldots

Using the commutation estimates for LP projections, the error terms that contain
\Phi
have good
2^{-k}
weights.

For each
P_{k}
projection we also consider the high frequency regime
\tau\in[2^{-k-1},1]
in Section
3.3.2
. We prove boundedness for the energy obtained by using the multiplier
2^{k}\tau\nabla_{\tau}
to get schematically for
\tau\in[2^{-k-1},1]:

\displaystyle 2^{k}\tau\big\|P_{k}\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}2^{k}\tau\big\|P_{k}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{i}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\ldots\lesssim\big\|P_{k}\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}\big|_{\tau=2^{-k-1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|P_{k}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{i}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}\big|_{\tau=2^{-k-1}}+

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{I}\int_{2^{-k-1}}^{\tau}2^{k}\tau^{\prime}\big\|P_{k}F_{M}^{i}\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}d\tau^{\prime}+\int_{2^{-k-1}}^{\tau}(\tau^{\prime})^{3}\big\|\underline{\widetilde{P}}_{k}\nabla\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}d\tau^{\prime}+\ldots

It is essential that in this estimate we obtain a top order bulk term with favorable sign, which can be dropped from the estimate
2
2

2

On the other hand, we point out that in the context of the second model system, the bulk term obtained in the high frequency regime estimate for the singular component has an unfavorable sign, creating major difficulties.
. We also notice that to deal with the error terms we use refined LP commutation estimates such as (
1.20
). In particular, this creates error terms with different projection operators, such as the last term written above, which cannot be controlled directly at the level of the high frequency regime estimates.

To conclude the proof of the top order estimates for the regular components, we combine the high frequency regime and low frequency regime estimates in Section
3.3.4
. We point out that this argument uses the top order estimates for the singular component, which are proved separately as we explain below. To bound the error terms containing different LP projection operators, we first need to sum the estimates obtained for all
k\geq 0,
and then use Gronwall. For the negative frequencies we use the lower order estimates from Section
3.2
. As a result, we obtain:

\tau\big\|(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J}\big\|^{2}_{H^{3/2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\tau\big\|\nabla^{M+1}\Phi_{i}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}}+\ldots\leq C_{I}\mathcal{D}_{I}+C_{I}\mathcal{F}_{I}(\tau).

Top order estimates for the singular component.

The singular component decouples from the rest of the system, so it can be treated independently of the regular quantities. We proved top order estimates for the singular component in
[
Cic24
]
, in order to illustrate the methods of the present paper. According to
[
Cic24
]
, we have:

\displaystyle\tau^{2}\big\|\nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}}+\tau^{2}\big\|\nabla\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}}\leq C_{I}\big\|\mathcal{O}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}},

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}\leq C_{I}\big(1+|\log\tau|^{2}\big)\big\|\mathcal{O}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}}.

In Section
3.3.3
we explain the proof in
[
Cic24
, Section 7]
, which follows similar steps to the above argument for the regular components and it relies on the lower order estimates for the singular component in Section
3.2
. In the high frequency regime there is the additional simplification of not having inhomogeneous terms on the RHS. In the low frequency regime we need to account for the singular behavior of the solution at
\tau=0,
so we prove estimates for
P_{k}\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}/\log(2^{k}\tau)
instead. The error terms can be simplified significantly using the additional lower order estimates in Section
3.2
. Finally, we combine the low frequency and high frequency regime estimates in a similar way to the regular components.

1.1.4
The proof of Theorem
1.2

In Theorem
1.2
, we obtain estimates for solutions of the second model system at
\tau\in(0,1)
in terms of the solution at
\tau=1.
Additionally, we also prove estimates for the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}.
In this section, we provide a detailed outline of the proof of Theorem
1.2
, and refer the reader to this section for assistance when reading the proof in Section
4
. Similarly to Section
1.1.3
, our strategy is to use the geometric LP theory and adapt the approach in
[
Cic23
]
to the current setting. We first explain how to prove estimates for the regular quantities. We then outline the lower order estimates and top order estimates for the singular quantities, and we also explain the estimates for the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-},
completing the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Estimates for the regular quantities.

The second model system has a favorable structure for proving estimates for the regular quantities
\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{I}
in terms of the initial data at
\tau=1,
due to the choice of sign
\sigma=2
in Definition
1.1
. Another key feature of the second model system is that the singular terms
\psi\nabla^{m+1}\Phi_{0}
are absent from the RHS of (
1.8
). Thus, the equations for the regular quantities decouple from the singular quantities, and we can estimate them separately.

The favorable choice of sign
\sigma=2
allows us to use
\nabla_{\tau}
as a multiplier in (
1.8
). We obtain in Section
4.1
:

\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|\Phi_{i}\big\|_{H^{M+3/2}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{i}\big\|_{H^{1/2}}^{2}+\ldots\leq C_{II}\mathcal{D}_{II}+C_{II}\mathcal{F}_{II}(\tau).

(1.25)

Using the expansions satisfied by the regular quantities at
\tau=0,
we obtain for the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
:

\sum_{i=1}^{I}\big\|\Phi_{i}^{0}\big\|^{2}_{H^{M+1}}\leq C_{II}\mathcal{D}_{II}+C_{II}\mathcal{F}_{II}(0).

(1.26)

Estimates for the singular quantities

The structure of the equation (
1.7
) satisfied by the singular quantity
\Phi_{0}
creates significant new challenges compared to the case of the regular quantities. Additionally, we must prove estimates consistent with the expansion of
\Phi_{0}
at
\tau=0,
and obtain suitable bounds for
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathfrak{h}.

We start with a lower order estimate proved in Section
4.2
, which provides useful preliminary bounds for
\Phi_{0}
:

\sum_{m=0}^{M}\tau^{2}\big\|\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\tau^{2}\big\|\Phi_{0}\big\|_{H^{M+1}}^{2}+\tau\big\|\Phi_{0}\big\|_{H^{M}}^{2}+\int_{\tau}^{1}\tau^{\prime}\big\|\Phi_{0}\big\|_{H^{M+1}}^{2}d\tau^{\prime}\leq C_{II}\mathcal{D}_{II}+C_{II}\mathcal{F}_{II}(\tau).

(1.27)

The main part of the argument consists of proving sharp estimates for the top order quantity
\xi=\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}.
We use as a guideline the toy problem considered in
[
Cic24
, Section 9]
, where we studied the equation satisfied by
\xi=\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0},
but we dropped the terms
F^{\prime}_{M}=F_{M}^{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}\psi\nabla^{M+1}\Phi_{i}
in order to illustrate the main ideas.

Similarly to Section
1.1.3
, we prove estimates for each projection
P_{k}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0},
with
k\geq 0.
For this purpose, we consider separately the low frequency regime
k\geq x
with
\tau\in[0,X2^{-k-1}],
and the high frequency regime
k\geq x
with
\tau\in[X2^{-k-1},1]
, where we introduced a large constant
X=2^{x+1}
for technical reasons. We note already here that we can deal with the terms with
k<x
using the preliminary estimate (
1.27
).

In Section
4.3
, we consider the low frequency regime
k\geq x,

\tau\in[0,X2^{-k-1}],
and we prove a similar estimate to (
1.27
) for
P_{k}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}:

\tau^{2}\big\|P_{k}\nabla_{\tau}\xi\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\tau^{2}\big\|\nabla P_{k}\xi\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim X^{2}2^{-2k}\Big(\big\|P_{k}\nabla_{\tau}\xi\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\big\|\nabla P_{k}\xi\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\Big)\Big|_{\tau=X2^{-k-1}}+C_{X}2^{-3k}\mathcal{F}_{II}(\tau)+\ldots

We note that the data terms at
\tau=X2^{-k-1}
will be bounded later using the high frequency regime estimates.

In Section
4.4
, we consider the high frequency regime
\tau\in[X2^{-k-1},1]
, and we prove a similar estimate to the one in Section
1.1.3
. Using the multiplier
2^{k}\tau\nabla_{\tau}
, we get:

2^{k}\tau\big\|P_{k}\xi\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}+\ldots\lesssim 2^{k}\big\|P_{k}\xi\big\|^{2}_{H^{1}}\big|_{\tau=1}+\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{2^{k}}{(\tau^{\prime})^{2}}\big\|P_{k}\xi\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}d\tau^{\prime}+\int_{\tau}^{1}2^{k}\tau^{\prime}\big\|P_{k}F_{M}^{\prime}\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}d\tau^{\prime}+\ldots

The main difference compared to Section
1.1.3
is the presence of the second term on the RHS above, which is a top order bulk term with an unfavorable sign, creating significant difficulties. We improve the above high frequency regime estimate and deal with this bad term in Section
4.5
, which represents the main technical part of the paper. We use the refined Poincaré inequality (
1.19
) to bound the error term:

\int_{\tau}^{1}\frac{2^{k}}{(\tau^{\prime})^{2}}\big\|P_{k}\xi\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}d\tau^{\prime}.

As a result, we obtain a sum of error terms on the RHS in the low frequency regime and high frequency regime. We bound the high frequency regime error terms using the novel Gronwall-like inequality in Lemma
4.1
. For the low frequency regime error terms we use the estimates of Section
4.3
, which in turn create a sum of error terms that we bound using the discrete Gronwall inequality.

Moreover, in the above estimates we also have error terms with different projection operators, obtained by using LP commutation estimates such as (
1.20
), similarly to Section
1.1.3
. As before, we deal with these terms towards the end of our argument, when summing the estimates obtained for all
k\geq x.

Finally, in Section
4.6
we combine the improved high frequency regime estimates of Section
4.5
with the low frequency regime estimates of Section
4.3
and the preliminary estimate (
1.27
), to obtain the main estimate for
\Phi_{0}:

\tau^{2}\big\|\Phi_{0}\big\|_{H^{M+3/2}}^{2}+\tau^{2}\big\|\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big\|_{H^{1/2}}^{2}+\ldots\leq C_{II}\mathcal{D}_{II}+C_{II}\mathcal{F}_{II}(\tau).

Using the estimate for the regular quantities (
1.25
) as well, we conclude the proof of the main estimate (
1.13
) in Theorem
1.2
.

Estimates for the asymptotic quantities

The final step in the proof of Theorem
1.2
is showing the estimates (
1.14
) and (
1.15
) for the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}
in Section
4.7
. In view of (
1.26
), to establish (
1.14
) we prove:

\|\mathcal{O}\|_{H^{M+1}}^{2}\leq C_{II}\mathcal{D}_{II}+C_{II}\mathcal{F}_{II}(0).

(1.28)

This estimate for the obstruction tensor follows by taking the limit
\tau\rightarrow 0
in the low frequency regime estimate above for each
k\geq x
, and using the expansion of
\Phi_{0}
at
\mathcal{I}^{-}.

The estimate (
1.15
) for
\mathfrak{h}
is more involved. We first notice that it suffices to show:

\sum_{k\geq x}2^{2k}\big\|P_{k}\mathfrak{h}_{M}\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{II}\mathcal{D}_{II}+C_{II}\mathcal{F}_{II}(0),

(1.29)

where
\mathfrak{h}_{M}=\nabla^{M}h-2(\log\nabla)\nabla^{M}\mathcal{O}.
According to the expansion of
\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}
at
\mathcal{I}^{-},
we can prove the above bound using energy estimates for the equations satisfied by the quantities
\overline{\xi}_{k}=P_{k}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}-\tau\log(2^{k}\tau)P_{k}\nabla_{\tau}\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}.
To deal with the error terms obtained in this case, we use the previously established bounds for the low frequency regime and the high frequency regime.

1.2
Outline of the paper

We outline the structure of the paper. In Section
2
we introduce the geometric Littlewood-Paley theory of
[
KR06
]
, and prove the additional new results needed in our analysis. In Section
3
, we prove Theorem
1.1
following the outline in Section
1.1.3
. In Section
4
, we prove Theorem
1.2
following the strategy presented in Section
1.1.4
.

Acknowledgements.

The author would like to acknowledge Igor Rodnianski and Mihalis Dafermos for their valuable advice in the process of writing this paper.

2
Geometric Littlewood-Paley Theory

We introduce the geometric Littlewood-Paley theory of Klainerman-Rodnianski
[
KR06
]
. We also use the methods of
[
KR06
,
KR05
]
to prove additional new results that are needed in the proofs of the sharp estimates in Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, as explained in Section
1.1.2
of the Introduction.

2.1
Bounds for the heat equation

The geometric Littlewood-Paley projections are defined using the heat flow. In this section, we introduce some standard properties of the heat equation based on
[
KR06
]
, and we prove additional commutation estimates. We notice that the estimates depend on the bounds assumed on the background spacetime (
1.5
) and (
1.6
). Unless otherwise noted, all the implicit constants present in the estimates depend only on the constant
C_{0}
.

For any tensor field
F
on
S_{\tau}
, we denote by
U(z)F
the solution on
[0,\infty)\times S_{\tau}
to the heat equation:

\partial_{z}U(z)F-\Delta U(z)F=0,\ U(0)F=F,

(2.1)

where
\Delta
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
\big(S_{\tau},\not{g}\big),
for some
\tau\in[0,1].
We notice that the operators
U
are self-adjoint and form a semigroup.

We use the following estimates for the heat kernel of
[
KR06
]
:

Proposition

(
[
KR06
, Proposition 4.1]
)
.

We have the estimates for the operator
U(z)
:

\displaystyle\big\|U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|F\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla F\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\big\|U(z)\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}z^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|F\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\big\|\Delta U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}z^{-1}\|F\|_{L^{2}}.

We prove additional estimates using the methods of
[
KR06
,
KR05
]
. In particular, we notice the importance of proving estimates for the commutation of the operator
U(z)
and the
e_{4}
vector field.

Notation.
Unless otherwise noted, in this section we write
A\lesssim B
for some quantities
A,B>0
if there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on the constant
N,C_{0}
defined in the Introduction, such that
A\leq CB.

Lemma 2.1
.

We have the estimates for the operator
U(z)
:

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla^{m}U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C\big(\|\not{Riem}\|_{H^{m-2}}\big)\cdot\|F\|_{H^{m}}

(2.2)

\displaystyle\big\|[U(z),\nabla]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\sqrt{z}+z)\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}

(2.3)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla[U(z),\nabla]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\sqrt{z})\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}

(2.4)

\displaystyle\big\|[U(z),\nabla^{m}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\sqrt{z}+z)C\big(\|\not{Riem}\|_{H^{m-1}}\big)\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{H^{m-1}}

(2.5)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla[U(z),\nabla^{m}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\sqrt{z}+z)C\big(\|\not{Riem}\|_{H^{m}}\big)\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{H^{m}}

(2.6)

\displaystyle\big\|[U(z),G]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(z+\sqrt{z})\big\|G\big\|_{W^{2,\infty}}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}

(2.7)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla[U(z),G]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\sqrt{z})\big\|G\big\|_{W^{2,\infty}}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}

(2.8)

\displaystyle\big\|[U(z),\nabla_{4}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+z)\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}

(2.9)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla[U(z),\nabla_{4}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+z)\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1}}

(2.10)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla^{2}U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(1+z^{-3/4}\big)\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1/2}}.

(2.11)

Proof.

We prove (
2.2
) by induction, while considering separately the cases
m\leq 2n
and
m>2n.
The case
m=1
is proved in
[
KR06
]
. We assume at first that (
2.2
) holds up to
m<2n,
and prove it for
m+1.
We notice that for any tensor
\phi
we have:

\|\nabla^{2}\phi\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\Delta\phi\|_{L^{2}}+\|\not{Riem}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\phi\|_{H^{1}}.

As a result, we get:

\|\nabla^{m+1}U(z)F\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\Delta\nabla^{m-1}U(z)F\|_{L^{2}}+\|\not{Riem}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla^{m-1}U(z)F\|_{H^{1}}.

Since
\|\not{Riem}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
by the bound on the background spacetime in (
1.5
), the second term is controlled using the induction hypothesis.
For the first term, we write using
[\Delta,U(z)]F=0
:

\|\Delta\nabla^{m-1}U(z)F\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\nabla^{m-1}\Delta U(z)F\|_{L^{2}}+\big\|[\Delta,\nabla^{m-1}]U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\lesssim\|\nabla^{m-1}U(z)\Delta F\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{i+2j=m-1}\big\|\nabla^{i}(\not{Riem}^{j+1}U(z)F)\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|F\|_{H^{m+1}},

by using (
1.5
) again. This completes the first induction argument, and establishes (
2.2
) for all
m\leq 2n.
Next, for some
m\geq 2n
we assume that (
2.2
) holds up to
m,
and prove it for
m+1.
As before, we have:

\|\nabla^{m+1}U(z)F\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\nabla^{m-1}U(z)\Delta F\|_{L^{2}}+\big\|[\Delta,\nabla^{m-1}]U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}+C\big(\|\not{Riem}\|_{H^{m-2}}\big)\cdot\|F\|_{H^{m}}

\lesssim C\big(\|\not{Riem}\|_{H^{m-2}}\big)\cdot\|F\|_{H^{m+1}}+\sum_{i+2j=m-1}\big\|\nabla^{i}(\not{Riem}^{j+1}U(z)F)\big\|_{L^{2}}.

The second term in the above can be written as:

\sum_{i+2j=m-1}\sum_{i_{0}+\ldots+i_{j+1}=i}\bigg\|\big(\nabla^{i_{0}}U(z)F\big)\prod_{l=1}^{j+1}\big(\nabla^{i_{l}}\not{Riem}\big)\bigg\|_{L^{2}}.

For each terms of the sum, we bound the factor with the most number of angular derivatives in
L^{2}.
The other factors have at most
(m-1)/2
derivatives, so we bound them in
L^{\infty}
and apply the Sobolev inequality to get:

\|\nabla^{m+1}U(z)F\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C\big(\|\not{Riem}\|_{H^{m-1}}\big)\cdot\|F\|_{H^{m+1}}.

This completes the proof of (
2.2
). Next, we prove (
2.3
). By Duhamel’s formula as in
[
KR05
]
we have:

[U(z),\nabla]F=\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})[\Delta,\nabla]U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}.

Using the standard estimates for the heat kernel in
[
KR06
]
, we get (
2.3
). The proof of (
2.4
) is similar. We also have using
[\Delta,\nabla^{m}]=\nabla[\Delta,\nabla^{m-1}]+[\Delta,\nabla]\nabla^{m-1}
:

[U(z),\nabla^{m}]F=\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})[\Delta,\nabla^{m}]U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}=\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})\nabla\big(\not{Riem}\nabla^{m-1}+[\Delta,\nabla^{m-1}]\big)U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}+

+\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})\big(\nabla\not{Riem}\cdot\nabla^{m-1}U(z^{\prime})F\big)dz^{\prime}.

This implies (
2.5
) using the bounds in (
2.2
), and by estimating the commutator term as in the proof of (
2.2
). The proof of (
2.6
) is similar. The proofs of (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) follow, since according to
[
KR06
]
:

[U(z),G]F=\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})\big(\Delta G\cdot U(z^{\prime})F+2\nabla G\cdot\nabla U(z^{\prime})F\big)dz^{\prime}.

By Duhamel’s formula as in
[
KR05
]
we have:

[U(z),\nabla_{4}]F=\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})[\Delta,\nabla_{4}]U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}.

Using (
2.13
) and the standard estimates for the heat kernel in
[
KR06
]
, we get (
2.9
). The proof of (
2.10
) is similar. Finally, to prove (
2.11
):

\big\|\nabla^{2}U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\big\|P_{k}\nabla^{2}U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim(1+z^{-1})\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{k\geq 0}\big\|\nabla^{2}P_{k}U(z)F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim

\lesssim(1+z^{-1})\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{k\geq 0}\big\|\nabla^{2}U(z)P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim(1+z^{-1})\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{-1}\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\cdot z^{-1/2}\big\|\nabla P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim

\lesssim(1+z^{-1})\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{-3/2}2^{k}\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim(1+z^{-1})\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+z^{-3/2}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1/2}}^{2}.

We note that the proof of the last statement uses parts of Lemma
2.3
. We included its statement in the heat equation bounds section for future convenience.
∎

In our proofs we will repeatedly use the commutation formulas in the following lemma. Their derivation is standard and is contained in Section 2 of
[
Cic24
]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

We have the commutation formulas:

\displaystyle[\nabla,\nabla_{4}]\phi

\displaystyle=\nabla\chi\cdot\phi+\chi\cdot\nabla\phi,

(2.12)

\displaystyle[\Delta,\nabla_{4}]\phi

\displaystyle=\nabla(\chi\nabla\phi)+O\big(\|\chi\|_{W^{2,\infty}}(|\nabla\phi|+|\phi|)\big).

(2.13)

2.2
Bounds for the LP projections

In this section, we follow
[
KR06
]
to define the LP projections using the heat flow. We then prove a series of additional bounds for the LP projections that are needed in our analysis.

The class
\mathcal{M}
of smooth symbols defined in
[
KR06
]
consists of smooth functions
m:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R},
which decay at infinity and satisfy the vanishing moments property:

\int_{0}^{\infty}z^{k_{1}}\partial_{z}^{k_{2}}m(z)dz=0,\ |k_{1}|+|k_{2}|\leq K,

for large enough order
K>0.
For any
m\in\mathcal{M}
, we set
m_{k}(z)=2^{2k}m(2^{2k}z).
For any tensor field
F
on
S_{\tau}
, we define the LP projections for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
:

P_{k}F=\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)U(z)Fdz.

We refer the reader to Theorem 5.5 in
[
KR06
]
for the fundamental properties of these operators, similar to the standard LP projections. We use the following estimates for the LP projections of
[
KR06
]
:

Proposition

(
[
KR06
, Theorem 5.5, Remark 5.6]
)
.

For an arbitrary LP projection, and any smooth tensor
F
we have:

1.

Bessel inequality.

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

2.

Finite band property.

\big\|\nabla P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}},\ \big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{-k}\big\|\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\big\|\Delta P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim 2^{2k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}},\ \big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{-2k}\big\|\Delta F\big\|_{L^{2}}

3.

L^{2}
-almost orthogonality. For any two families of LP projections
P_{k},\widetilde{P}_{k}
we have:

\big\|P_{k}\widetilde{P}_{k^{\prime}}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{-4|k-k^{\prime}|}\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}

Using the geometric LP projections, we can define fractional Sobolev spaces. We first state the following result of
[
KR06
]
:

Proposition

(
[
KR06
, Corollary 7.12]
)
.

For an arbitrary LP projection,
a\geq 0
and any smooth tensor
F,
we have:

\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{2ak}\big\|P_{k}F\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{H^{a}}^{2}.

Moreover, if
\sum_{k}P_{k}^{2}=I
and
a<4,
then:

\big\|F\big\|_{H^{a}}^{2}\lesssim\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{2ak}\big\|P_{k}F\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

We use this result to give an equivalent definition of fractional Sobolev spaces:

Definition 2.1
.

Let
P_{k}
be a family of projections with
\sum_{k}P_{k}^{2}=I
. We write any
a\geq 0
as
a=[a]+\{a\},
with
\{a\}\in[0,1).
For any smooth tensor
F
we define its Sobolev norm of order
a
as:

\big\|F\big\|_{H^{a}}^{2}:=\big\|F\big\|_{H^{[a]}}^{2}+\big\|\nabla^{[a]}F\big\|_{H^{\{a\}}}^{2},

where we define the
H^{\{a\}}
Sobolev norm by:

\big\|F\big\|_{H^{\{a\}}}^{2}:=\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{2\{a\}k}\big\|P_{k}F\big\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Following the ideas of
[
KR06
]
, we prove additional commutation bounds. As before, we highlight the importance of proving estimates for the commutation with
e_{4},
which allow us to control the change of the projection operators in terms of the time
\tau.
Moreover, we notice that the following bounds rely on our previous estimates for the heat flow in Lemma
2.1
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The LP projection operators satisfy the following bounds for
k\geq 0
:

\displaystyle[\nabla_{t},P_{k}]

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{2^{2k-1}}[\nabla_{4},P_{k}],\text{ where }t=2^{k}\tau

(2.14)

\displaystyle\big\|[\nabla^{m},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-k}C\big(\|\not{Riem}\|_{H^{m-1}}\big)\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{H^{m-1}}

(2.15)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla[\nabla^{m},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-k}C\big(\|\not{Riem}\|_{H^{m}}\big)\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{H^{m}}

(2.16)

\displaystyle\big\|[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}

(2.17)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1}}

(2.18)

\displaystyle\big\|[P_{k},G]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-k}\big\|G\big\|_{W^{2,\infty}}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}

(2.19)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla[P_{k},G]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|G\big\|_{W^{2,\infty}}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

(2.20)

Proof.

The first formula follows since
\nabla_{t}=2^{-k}\nabla_{\tau}=2^{-k+1}\tau\nabla_{4}=2^{-2k+1}t\nabla_{4}.
In order to prove (
2.15
), we notice that using the bound in (
2.5
) we have that:

\big\|[\nabla^{m},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}|\cdot\big\|[\nabla^{m},U(z)]F\big\|_{L^{2}}dz\lesssim 2^{-k}C\big(\|\not{Riem}\|_{H^{m-1}}\big)\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{H^{m-1}}.

Similarly, we also have that:

\big\|\nabla[\nabla^{m},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}|\cdot\big\|\nabla[\nabla^{m},U(z)]F\big\|_{L^{2}}dz\lesssim 2^{-k}C\big(\|\not{Riem}\|_{H^{m}}\big)\cdot\big\|F\big\|_{H^{m}}.

Next, we have that:

\big\|[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}|\cdot\big\|[\nabla_{4},U(z)]F\big\|_{L^{2}}dz\leq\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

Similarly, we also have that:

\big\|\nabla[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}|\cdot\big\|\nabla[\nabla_{4},U(z)]F\big\|_{L^{2}}dz\leq\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1}}.

The proofs of (
2.19
) and (
2.20
) follow from (
2.7
) and (
2.8
).
∎

One disadvantage of the estimates (
2.17
) and (
2.18
) is that the right hand side cannot be summed in
k,
whereas the left hand side can be summed because of the presence of the
P_{k}
operator. We address this by proving a refined version of these estimates, which contains a different projection operator on the right hand side. The presence of different projection operators will pose additional difficulties in the analysis of the model systems. We notice that this issue is a consequence of the nontrivial time dependence of the metrics
\not{g}.

We define the symbol
\widetilde{m}\in\mathcal{M}
given by
\widetilde{m}(z)=zm(z),
and we denote by
\widetilde{P}_{k}
the associated projection operator. Moreover, we also introduce the projection operator
\underline{\widetilde{P}}_{k}
which satisfies
\underline{\widetilde{P}}_{k}^{2}=\widetilde{P}_{k}.

Lemma 2.4
.

We have the following estimates for
k\geq 0
:

\displaystyle[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F

\displaystyle=2^{-2k}\chi\nabla^{2}\widetilde{P}_{k}F+O\Big(2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}\Big)

(2.21)

\displaystyle\big\|[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|\underline{\widetilde{P}}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}+2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}

(2.22)

\displaystyle\nabla[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F

\displaystyle=2^{-2k}\chi\nabla^{2}\widetilde{P}_{k}\nabla F+O\Big(2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1}}\Big)

(2.23)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|\underline{\widetilde{P}}_{k}\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}+2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1}}.

(2.24)

Proof.

In order to prove (
2.21
), we compute the following:

[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F=\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)[\nabla_{4},U(z)]Fdz=\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})[\nabla_{4},\Delta]U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}dz

=\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})\nabla\big(\chi\nabla U(z^{\prime})F\big)+\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})\Big([\nabla_{4},\Delta]U(z^{\prime})F-\nabla\big(\chi\nabla U(z^{\prime})F\big)\Big).

We can bound the second term in
L^{2}
by:

\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}\big\|U(z^{\prime})F\big\|_{H^{1}}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}(z^{\prime})^{-1/2}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim 2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

Next, we write the first term as:

\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla U(z-z^{\prime})\big(\chi\nabla U(z^{\prime})F\big)+\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}[U(z-z^{\prime}),\nabla]\big(\chi\nabla U(z^{\prime})F\big).

(2.25)

The second term in (
2.25
) can be bounded in
L^{2}
by:

\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}(z-z^{\prime})^{1/2}\big\|\chi\nabla U(z^{\prime})F\big\|_{L^{2}}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}(z-z^{\prime})^{1/2}(z^{\prime})^{-1/2}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim 2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

We write the first term in (
2.25
) as:

\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla\big(\chi\cdot U(z-z^{\prime})\nabla U(z^{\prime})F\big)+\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla[U(z-z^{\prime}),\chi]\big(\nabla U(z^{\prime})F\big).

The latter term in the above is bounded in
L^{2}
by:

\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}\big\|\nabla U(z^{\prime})F\big\|_{L^{2}}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}(z^{\prime})^{-1/2}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim 2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

As a result, we proved that:

[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F=\chi\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla U(z-z^{\prime})\nabla U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}dz+O\Big(2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}\Big)

=\chi\cdot\nabla^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}zm_{k}(z)U(z)Fdz+\chi\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla[U(z-z^{\prime}),\nabla]U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}dz+O\Big(2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}\Big)

=2^{-2k}\chi\cdot\nabla^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{m}_{k}(z)U(z)Fdz+O\Big(2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}\Big)=2^{-2k}\chi\nabla^{2}\widetilde{P}_{k}F+O\Big(2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}\Big).

The proof of (
2.22
) follows from (
2.21
), since
\underline{\widetilde{P}}_{k}^{2}=\widetilde{P}_{k}.

We now prove (
2.23
). We notice that using (
2.13
) we can write:

\nabla[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F=\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla U(z-z^{\prime})[\nabla_{4},\Delta]U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}dz

=\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla U(z-z^{\prime})\big(\chi\nabla^{2}U(z^{\prime})F\big)+\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla U(z-z^{\prime})\Big([\nabla_{4},\Delta]U(z^{\prime})F-\chi\nabla^{2}U(z^{\prime})F\Big).

The second term can be bounded in
L^{2}
by:

\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}(z-z^{\prime})^{-1/2}\big\|U(z^{\prime})F\big\|_{H^{1}}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim 2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1}}.

The first term in the above expression can be written as:

\chi\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla U(z-z^{\prime})\nabla^{2}U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}dz+\nabla\chi\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})\nabla^{2}U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}dz+

+\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla[U(z-z^{\prime}),\chi]\nabla^{2}U(z^{\prime})Fdz^{\prime}dz.

To complete the proof of (
2.23
), we need to write this expression as
2^{-2k}\chi\nabla^{2}\widetilde{P}_{k}\nabla F+O\big(2^{-k}\|F\|_{H^{1}}\big)
. Indeed, we can bound the last two terms in
L^{2}
by:

\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}\big\|\nabla^{2}U(z^{\prime})F\big\|_{L^{2}}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}\big\|\nabla[\nabla,U(z^{\prime})]F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}\big\|\nabla U(z^{\prime})\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\lesssim 2^{-2k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}+2^{-k}\big\|\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

Finally, the leading term can be written as:

\chi\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla U(z-z^{\prime})\nabla U(z^{\prime})\nabla F+\chi\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla U(z-z^{\prime})\nabla[\nabla,U(z^{\prime})]F=

=\chi\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}\nabla U(z-z^{\prime})\nabla U(z^{\prime})\nabla F+O\Big(2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}\Big)=2^{-2k}\chi\nabla^{2}\widetilde{P}_{k}\nabla F+O\Big(2^{-k}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1}}\Big),

where in the last step we used the same argument as in the proof of (
2.21
). This completes the proof of (
2.23
). Moreover, the proof of (
2.24
) follows from (
2.23
) as before.
∎

We also prove estimates where we trade
1/2
derivatives on
F
for
2^{k/2}
growth, which simplify certain error terms in the analysis of the top order singular component of the first model system in the low frequency regime:

Lemma 2.5
.

We have the following estimates for
k\geq 0
:

\big\|[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{k/2}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1/2}}

(2.26)

\big\|\nabla[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{k/2}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1/2}}.

(2.27)

Proof.

In the previous proof we obtained the identity:

[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]\nabla F=\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})\nabla\big(\chi\nabla U(z^{\prime})\nabla F\big)+

+\int_{0}^{\infty}m_{k}(z)\int_{0}^{z}U(z-z^{\prime})\Big([\nabla_{4},\Delta]U(z^{\prime})\nabla F-\nabla\big(\chi\nabla U(z^{\prime})\nabla F\big)\Big).

As a result, we get from Lemma
2.1
:

\big\|[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}\big((z-z^{\prime})^{-1/2}+1\big)\big\|\nabla U(z^{\prime})\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}dz^{\prime}dz

\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}\big((z-z^{\prime})^{-1/2}+1\big)(z^{\prime})^{-3/4}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1/2}}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1/2}}\bigg(1+\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|z^{-1/4}dz\bigg).

A similar proof also gives:

\big\|\nabla[\nabla_{4},P_{k}]F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}(z)|\int_{0}^{z}(z-z^{\prime})^{-1/2}\big\|\nabla^{2}U(z^{\prime})F\big\|_{L^{2}}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim 2^{k/2}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{1/2}}.

∎

Convention.
For the remainder of the paper, we fix the projection operator
P_{k}
to satisfy
\sum_{k}P_{k}^{2}=I.
We notice that all the estimates established above in this section are valid for any LP projections with symbols in
\mathcal{M}
.

In order to control a top order bulk term with bad sign in the high frequency estimate for the second model system, we need a refined Poincaré inequality for LP projections. The key aspect of this result is that the projection operators on the right hand side have the same symbol as the one on the left hand side. Moreover, all the frequencies higher than
k
are contained in the last term, which is lower order.

Lemma 2.6
.

For any
k\geq 0,
and
\delta>0
, we have the inequality:

\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\delta}2^{-2k}\big\|\nabla P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\delta\sum_{0\leq l<k}2^{-9k+7l}\big\|\nabla P_{l}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\delta^{-1}2^{-4k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(2.28)

Proof.

Let
\dot{P}
be the projection operator with symbol given by
\dot{m}(z)=-\int_{z}^{\infty}m(z^{\prime})dz^{\prime}.
According to the proof of
[
KR06
, Theorem 5.5 (v)]
we have that
2^{2k}P_{k}F=\Delta\dot{P}_{k}F.
This implies the Poincaré inequality:

\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\delta}2^{-k}\big\|\nabla\dot{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-k}\big\|\nabla P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

Also, for all
l\geq k\geq 0
we have the following estimate, according to the proof of
[
KR06
, Theorem 5.5 (ii)]
:

\big\|P_{l}\dot{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{-2(l-k)}\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

We use these two bounds, together with the other usual bounds for LP projections, to get:

\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\delta}2^{-k}\big\|\nabla\dot{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-k}\big\|\nabla P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\delta}2^{-k}\big\|\dot{P}_{k}\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-2k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-k}\big\|\nabla P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\lesssim\sqrt{\delta}\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{-k}\big\|P_{l}^{2}\dot{P}_{k}\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-2k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-k}\big\|\nabla P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-k}\big\|P_{k}\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\sqrt{\delta}\sum_{0\leq l<k}2^{-k-4|k-l|}\big\|P_{l}\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-2k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-k}\big\|\nabla P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-k}\big\|\nabla P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\sqrt{\delta}\sum_{0\leq l<k}2^{-|k-l|/2}\cdot 2^{-k-7|k-l|/2}\big\|P_{l}\nabla F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}2^{-2k}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}}.

We square this inequality and use Cauchy-Schwarz in order to conclude.
∎

We introduce the
(\log\nabla)
operator, essential for the renormalization of
h
in (
1.10
). We define for any smooth tensor
F
on
S_{0}
and
k\geq 0
:

(\log\nabla)F=\sum_{l\geq 0}P_{l}^{2}F\cdot\log 2^{l}.

We prove the following bound on the
(\log\nabla)
operator, used in lower order estimates:

Lemma 2.7
.

Set
\eta=1/10.
For any smooth horizontal tensor
F,
and any
s\geq 0,
we have the estimate:

\big\|(\log\nabla)F\big\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{H^{s+\eta}}.

Proof.

We first notice that we have the inequality:

\big\|(\log\nabla)F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{k\geq 0}\big\|P_{k}^{2}F\cdot\log 2^{k}\big\|_{L^{2}}\bigg)^{2}\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k\eta/2}\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\bigg)^{2}\lesssim\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{2k\eta}\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{H^{\eta}}.

Using this, we can also bound the following:

\big\|(\log\nabla)F\big\|_{H^{s}}^{2}\lesssim\big\|(\log\nabla)F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{l\geq 0}2^{2sl}\big\|P_{l}(\log\nabla)F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\big\|(\log\nabla)F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{l\geq 0}2^{2sl}\big\|(\log\nabla)P_{l}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{H^{\eta}}+\sum_{l,k\geq 0}2^{2sl}2^{2\eta k}\big\|P_{k}P_{l}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{H^{\eta}}+\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{l=0}^{k}2^{2(s+\eta)k}\big\|P_{l}P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{l\geq 0}\sum_{k=0}^{l}2^{2(s+\eta)l}\big\|P_{k}P_{l}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{H^{\eta}}+\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{2(s+\eta)k}\big\|P_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\big\|F\big\|_{H^{s+\eta}}.

∎

Next, we define the following operator for
k\geq 0
:

R_{k}F=2P_{k}(\log\nabla)F-2\log 2^{k}\cdot P_{k}F=2\sum_{l\geq 0}\log 2\cdot(l-k)\cdot P_{k}P_{l}^{2}F-2\sum_{l<0}\log 2^{k}\cdot P_{k}P_{l}^{2}F.

(2.29)

The operator
R_{k}
appears as a commutation error term when projecting the expansion at
\{\tau=0\}
of the singular component of
\Phi_{0}
in the analysis of the first model system.

We consider the projection operator
\underline{P}_{k}
which satisfies
\underline{P}_{k}^{2}=P_{k}.
We have the estimates for
R_{k}:

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
F
be any smooth tensor on
S_{0}
. We extend
R_{k}F
to
(0,1]\times S^{n}
to be independent of
\tau.
We also denote
t=2^{k}\tau.
Then, for any
k\geq 0
we have:

\displaystyle\big\|\Delta_{\not{g}_{\tau}}R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{k}\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}}

(2.30)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}}

(2.31)

\displaystyle 2^{k}\big\|R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}}

(2.32)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla_{t}R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-3k}t\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}}

(2.33)

\displaystyle\big\|\nabla\nabla_{t}R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-2k}t\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}}.

(2.34)

Proof.

Because of (
1.5
), we can express the LHS of (
2.30
) using derivatives at
\{\tau=0\}
:

2^{-k}\big\|\Delta_{\not{g}_{\tau}}R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 2^{-k}\big\|R_{k}F\big\|_{H^{2}(S_{0})}\lesssim 2^{-k}\big\|\Delta_{\not{g}_{0}}R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}(S_{0})}+2^{-k}\big\|R_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}(S_{0})}

Using the finite band property of
[
KR06
]
we have:

2^{k}\big\|R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}(S_{0})}\lesssim\sum_{l\geq 0}2^{k}|l-k|\cdot\big\|\underline{P}_{k}P_{l}P_{l}\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{l<0}k2^{k}\cdot\big\|\underline{P}_{k}P_{l}P_{l}\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim

\lesssim\sum_{l\geq 0}\frac{2^{k-l}|l-k|}{2^{2|l-k|}}\cdot\big\|\nabla\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{l<0}k2^{-k+2l}\cdot\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}(S_{0})},

2^{-k}\big\|\Delta_{\not{g}_{0}}R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{l\geq 0}2^{-k}|l-k|\cdot\big\|\underline{P}_{k}P_{l}\Delta_{\not{g}_{0}}P_{l}\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{l<0}k2^{-k}\cdot\big\|\underline{P}_{k}P_{l}\Delta_{\not{g}_{0}}P_{l}\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim

\lesssim\sum_{l\geq 0}\frac{2^{l-k}|l-k|}{2^{2|l-k|}}\cdot\big\|\nabla\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}+\sum_{l<0}k2^{-3k+l}\cdot\big\|\nabla\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}(S_{0})}.

Combining these two estimates, we also have that:

\big\|\nabla R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}(S_{0})}\lesssim\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}(S_{0})}.

So far we proved (
2.30
), (
2.31
), and (
2.32
). In order to prove (
2.33
) and (
2.34
), we notice that for any horizontal
k
-tensor
\Phi
we have the formula:

\nabla_{\tau}\Phi=\mathcal{L}_{\tau}\Phi+\tau\chi\cdot\Phi.

(2.35)

Using this, we get:

\big\|\nabla_{t}R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\big\|(\nabla_{t}-\mathcal{L}_{t})R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{t}{2^{2k}}\big\|R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{t}{2^{3k}}\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}(S_{0})}.

Finally, we have:

\big\|\nabla\nabla_{t}R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\big\|\nabla(\nabla_{t}-\mathcal{L}_{t})R_{k}F\big\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\frac{t}{2^{2k}}\big\|R_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\frac{t}{2^{2k}}\big\|\underline{P}_{k}F\big\|_{H^{1}(S_{0})}.

∎

Finally, we prove the following result which implies that it is equivalent whether we project the expansions at
\tau=0
with respect to
\not{g}_{0}=\not{g}(0)
or
\not{g}_{\tau}=\not{g}(\tau).

Lemma 2.9
.

We consider
F
to be a smooth tensor on
S_{0},
extended to be independent of
\tau.
Denote by
{}^{(\not{g}_{0})}P_{k}
the projection with respect to
\big(S^{n}_{0},\not{g}_{0}\big)
and by
{}^{(\not{g}_{\tau})}P_{k}
the projection with respect to
\big(S^{n}_{\tau},\not{g}_{\tau}\big)
. Then we have the estimate for any
s\geq 0,\ k\geq 0
:

\big\|\big(\ ^{(\not{g}_{0})}P_{k}-\ ^{(\not{g}_{\tau})}P_{k}\big)F\big\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim_{s}\tau^{2}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{s+2}}.

(2.36)

Proof.

The bound follows using Duhamel’s formula as in
[
KR05
]
:

\big\|\big(\ ^{(\not{g}_{0})}P_{k}-\ ^{(\not{g}_{\tau})}P_{k}\big)F\big\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}|\cdot\big\|\ ^{(\not{g}_{0})}U(z)F-\ ^{(\not{g}_{\tau})}U(z)F\big\|_{H^{s}}dz

\lesssim\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}|\int_{0}^{z}\Big\|\ ^{(\not{g}_{0})}U(z-z^{\prime})\big(\Delta_{\not{g}_{\tau}}-\Delta_{\not{g}_{0}}\big)\ ^{(\not{g}_{\tau})}U(z^{\prime})F\Big\|_{H^{s}}dz^{\prime}dz\lesssim_{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}|m_{k}|\int_{0}^{z}\tau^{2}\big\|\ ^{(\not{g}_{\tau})}U(z^{\prime})F\big\|_{H^{s+2}}dz^{\prime}dz,

where we also used the expansion of
\not{g}_{\tau}
at
\tau=0.
The last term is bounded by
\tau^{2}\big\|F\big\|_{H^{s+2}}
.
∎

3
The First Model System

In this section we prove Theorem
1.1
, obtaining estimates for solutions of the first model system at
\tau\in(0,1]
in terms of the asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}.
We follow the strategy from Section
1.1.3
of the Introduction, and we advise the reader to refer to the outline for assistance while reading the proof below.

We recall the decomposition of
\Phi_{0}
into its singular and regular components. For each
m\leq M
we have:

\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}=\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}+\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{J},

where each singular component
\big(\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}
satisfies (
1.21
) and decouples from the rest of the system. In Section
3.1
we prove the existence and uniqueness of the singular component.

We prove estimates separately for the regular quantities
(\Phi_{0})_{J},\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{I}
and the singular quantity
(\Phi_{0})_{Y}.
In Section
3.2
we prove the lower order estimates outlined in Section
1.1.3
. We prove (
1.23
) in Proposition
3.3
and (
1.24
) in Proposition
3.2
. We also prove estimates for the commutator term
\mathcal{C}=\big(\nabla^{M}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}-\nabla\big(\nabla^{M-1}\Phi_{0}\big)_{Y}
and
H^{1/2}
estimates for
\nabla(\nabla^{M-1}\Phi_{0})_{Y}
and
\nabla_{\tau}(\nabla^{M-1}\Phi_{0})_{Y}
in Section
3.2.2
.

In Section
3.3
we prove the top order estimates outlined in Section
1.1.3
. We prove the low frequency regime estimates for the regular quantities in Section
3.3.1
, and the corresponding high frequency regime estimates in Section
3.3.2
. In Section
3.3.3
we state the top order estimates for the singular component which are proved in
[
Cic24
, Section 7]
. Finally, we combine the estimates in Section
3.3.4
to complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Notation.
Unless otherwise noted, in this section we write
A\lesssim B
for some quantities
A,B>0
if there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on the constants
M,C_{0},C_{1}
defined in the Introduction, such that
A\leq CB.

3.1
Construction of the singular component

In this section, we prove an existence and uniqueness result for solutions of (
3.1
) with asymptotic data at
\mathcal{I}^{-}.
In particular, this implies the existence and uniqueness of the singular component defined by (
1.21
).

We first remark that we frequently use
\nabla_{\tau}
as a multiplier to obtain energy estimates. The following lemma implies that the additional terms resulting from differentiating the volume form or the metric can be controlled using Gronwall for
\tau\in(0,1].
We point out that we usually bound these terms implicitly.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any smooth horizontal tensor
F
defined on
\mathcal{M},
we have:

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{d\tau}\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}(S_{\tau})}^{2}=\int_{S_{\tau}}F\cdot\nabla_{\tau}FdVol_{\not{g}}+O\Big(\tau\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}(S_{\tau})}^{2}\Big).

Proof.

We denote
v=\sqrt{\tau},
and compute that
\partial_{\tau}=2\tau\partial_{v}
and
e_{4}=\partial_{v}.
Since
\chi=\mathcal{L}_{e_{4}}\not{g},
we use the standard formula for
\frac{d}{dv}\int_{S^{n}}|F|^{2}(v)dVol_{\not{g}}
to get:

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{d\tau}\int_{S_{\tau}}|F|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{S_{\tau}}\nabla_{\tau}|F|^{2}+\int_{S_{\tau}}\tau\text{tr}\chi|F|^{2}=\int_{S_{\tau}}F\cdot\nabla_{\tau}F+O\Big(\tau\big\|F\big\|_{L^{2}(S_{\tau})}^{2}\Big).

∎

The following result implies that we can decompose the solution
\nabla^{m}\Phi_{0}
into its regular and singular components, for each
m\leq M.

Proposition 3.1
.

For any
\Phi^{0},\Phi^{1}\in C^{\infty}(S^{n})
smooth tensors, there exists a unique solution on
\mathcal{M}
of:

\nabla_{\tau}\big(\nabla_{\tau}\Phi\big)+\frac{1}{{\tau}}\nabla_{\tau}\Phi-4\Delta\Phi=\psi\nabla\Phi,

(3.1)
